    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\n(this is the start of the consultation)\n\n[Last Question]\nHello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Increased_Goal_Activity\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can’t seem to stop launching projects—work plans, certification schedules, home network upgrades, messages to old contacts—and it gets so intense that I blow past normal limits until my wife or my director has to step in.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for fitting me in. Um, I hope I can give you the right information today. 

I guess I'm mostly here because... well, my wife and my director at work sort of asked me to come. I've just been really, really driven lately. I mean, it's a good thing to be motivated, right? But I guess I've been launching a ton of new projects all at once. Like, drafting these massive new work plans, scheduling myself for a bunch of new IT certifications, completely upgrading our home network, messaging a lot of old contacts... just trying to get ahead and be productive, you know? 

But I guess it gets a little intense for the people around me. I sort of just keep going and blow past normal limits until my wife or my boss has to actually step in and tell me to stop. I really don't mean to cause any trouble or worry anyone, I just get really focused on getting things done. Does that make sense?

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for fitting me in. Um, I hope I can give you the right information today. \n\nI guess I'm mostly here because... well, my wife and my director at work sort of asked me to come. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

  "candidates": ["F30.1", "F30.2", "F31.1", "F31.2", "F25.0", "F90.9"],

This suggests a manic or hypomanic episode. Differential includes bipolar I disorder (current manic episode), bipolar II disorder (hypomanic episode), cyclothymia, schizoaffective disorder bipolar type, and ADHD as alternative explanation for hyperactivity/impulsivity.",
  "is_final": false

Um, well, when it hits, it usually sticks around for a while. Like... maybe a few months at a stretch, if I'm being honest. It doesn't really come and go day to day. It just sort of settles in.

I don't want to make it sound worse than it is, I just... I do get into these slumps sometimes. Are we talking about days, weeks, or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, um... if you look at the whole picture, like over the entire time I've had these ups and downs, I guess all those low, withdrawn periods have added up to... maybe about seven months total? \n\nI mean, it's definitely not all at once! Just, you know, a few weeks here or there that sort of accumulate over time. I really try my best to push through it and not let it affect my team or my marriage, so I hope it doesn't sound like I'm just sitting around doing nothing for months on end. I'd hate for you to think I'm just being lazy or not trying. \n\nIs... is that kind of timeline helpful for what you need to know? But, yeah, I suppose there are stretches where... where I just wake up feeling kind of like a failed version of myself. \n\nIt's not that bad, really, just this heavy, flat feeling. Like, even answering my wife's normal questions about the day just feels like it's asking too much of me. I try not to let it show because I don't want to be a burden, and I know I should be grateful for everything I have. I hope that's the kind of thing you were asking about? I don't want to make it sound worse than it is, I just... I do get into these slumps sometimes. Are we talking about days, weeks, or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, um... if you look at the whole picture, like over the entire time I've had these ups and downs, I guess all those low, withdrawn periods have added up to... maybe about seven months total? \n\nI mean, it's definitely not all at once! I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right details.\n\nDoctor: When you have one of those low, flat periods you mentioned, does it tend to last for a few weeks or more at a stretch before you start feeling better? Or is it more like the low mood comes and goes throughout each day or week?\n\nPatient: Um, well, when it hits, it usually sticks around for a while. Like... maybe a few months at a stretch, if I'm being honest. It doesn't really come and go day to day. It just sort of settles in. \n\nI guess the main thing is... well, I just stop doing the things I usually love. Like, I used to really look forward to grilling on the weekends, or going indoor climbing, or playing music at church. I even mentor the high school robotics kids, which I normally think is just the best thing. But during those long stretches, I just... I kind of stop caring about any of it. I'll see their messages asking for help or asking to hang out, and I just let them sit there unanswered. \n\nI feel terrible admitting that, because I know people are counting on me and I really don't want to let anyone down. I know I should just push through and go anyway. Does that make sense? I hope that's not too unusual or... or disappointing to hear.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those low periods that lasts a few months, is that one continuous stretch of feeling down, or does it break up into separate episodes with some better days or weeks in between?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I'm not entirely sure how to answer that. I mean, it's not like I'm tracking every single day on a calendar or anything. I try to stay positive and just keep moving forward, you know? \n\nI suppose... well, I don't really know if it's one long, unbroken stretch or if there are little breaks in there. only person who can see the whole architecture clearly and that if people would just follow my plan, I could save the company from a major failure.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: There have been runs where I sleep maybe three hours, wake up sharp at 3 or 4 a.m., and feel like I’ve been handed bonus time instead of feeling tired.\n- key: S038\n  name: Racing_Thoughts\n  description: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.\n  manifestation: My thoughts can move so fast that one idea opens ten more tabs in my head, and by the time I’m typing an email I’m already three solutions ahead and losing the original point.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: If a phone buzzes, a fan clicks, or someone walks past my office door, my attention snaps to it and I’ll abandon whatever report or ticket I was supposed to finish.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During my most driven stretches, when I was barely sleeping and pushing huge plans, I was also hearing comments in the server Does the mood improve over a day or two, or does it take longer—like a week or more—before you feel like yourself again?\n\nPatient: Um, well, when it starts to get better... Um, I think... well, it's not like it's always there, weighing me down completely. I really don't want to sound like I'm just miserable all the time, because I'm not. I have good days!

Um, I guess it's kind of like what I was just trying to explain... I really don't think it's always there underneath. I mean, when I'm feeling better, I really do feel good! I go to work, I manage my IT team, and I just try to be as productive and positive as I can be.

I'll see their messages asking for help or asking to hang out, and I just let them sit there unanswered. \n\nI feel terrible admitting that, because I know people are counting on me and I really don't want to let anyone down. I know I should just push through and go anyway. Does that make sense? I hope that's not too unusual or... or disappointing to hear.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those low periods that lasts a few months, is that one continuous stretch of feeling down, or does it break up into separate episodes with some better days or weeks in between?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I'm not entirely sure how to answer that. I mean, it's not like I'm tracking every single day on a calendar or anything. I try to stay positive and just keep moving forward, you know? \n\nI suppose... well, I don't really know if it's one long, unbroken stretch or if there are little breaks in there. I'd like to think I have some good days mixed in, because I really do try to snap out of it. I don't want to be difficult or give you the wrong impression. I just... I haven't really paid that close attention to whether it's continuous or broken up. Is it okay if I don't have a perfectly clear answer for that one? I want to be helpful, I just don't want to guess and tell you the wrong thing.\n\nDoctor: When you think back over the past year or two, can you describe how many separate times you've had one of those low, flat periods? Like, how many different times did it start and then eventually lift?\n\nPatient: Um, gosh... I really haven't kept a strict count. I mean, I try not to dwell on the negative times too much, so I just sort of push past them when they lift. \n\nIf I had to guess... I don't know, maybe a few times? But honestly, I'm just not certain how many separate times it's happened over the last year or two. I really wish I had a better answer for you. I should probably be keeping a journal or something, shouldn't I? I just... I haven't really tracked the exact number of times. Is it alright if I just say I'm not really sure?\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those high-energy, really driven periods you mentioned at the beginning—where you're launching lots of projects and feeling very motivated—how much sleep do you typically need during those times? Do you feel rested on just a few hours of sleep, or do you still feel like you need your normal amount?\n\nPatient: Well, um, when I get like that, I actually don't need a whole lot of sleep. I hope that doesn't sound too strange. But, yeah, there have been these runs where I'll sleep maybe... maybe three hours? And then I'll wake up sharp at 3 or 4 a.m., and I don't feel tired at all. It's actually kind of nice, like I've been handed bonus time to get things done, you know? \n\nI know you're supposed to get eight hours, and I really do try to have good habits most of the time. But during those stretches, I just feel completely rested on those few hours. Is that... is that something I should be worried about? I just want to make sure I'm not doing anything wrong by taking advantage of the extra time.\n\nDoctor: During one of those low periods you mentioned, when it does eventually lift and you start feeling better, how quickly does that happen? Does the mood improve over a day or two, or does it take longer—like a week or more—before you feel like yourself again?\n\nPatient: Um, well, when it starts to get better... I guess I haven't really timed it exactly. I try to just be grateful when the fog starts lifting, you know? \n\nI don't think it's like flipping a switch in a day or two, but... I also don't want to make it sound like it drags on forever once it starts improving. Maybe it's somewhere in the middle? I just sort of realize one day that things feel a little lighter, and I try to focus on that instead of analyzing how long it took. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's another vague answer. I really am trying to give you the information you need. Is it okay if I'm just not totally sure how fast the transition is? I hope I'm not being too frustrating.\n\nDoctor: When one of those low periods starts to lift, and you begin feeling better, would you say it takes at least two weeks before you're back to feeling like your normal self again?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm really not sure if it takes a full two weeks. Are we talking about days, weeks, or longer?\n\nPatient: Well, um... if you look at the whole picture, like over the entire time I've had these ups and downs, I guess all those low, withdrawn periods have added up to... maybe about seven months total? \n\nI mean, it's definitely not all at once! Just, you know, a few weeks here or there that sort of accumulate over time. I really try my best to push through it and not let it affect my team or my marriage, so I hope it doesn't sound like I'm just sitting around doing nothing for months on end. I'd hate for you to think I'm just being lazy or not trying. \n\nIs... is that kind of timeline helpful for what you need to know? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right details.\n\nDoctor: When you have one of those low, flat periods you mentioned, does it tend to last for a few weeks or more at a stretch before you start feeling better? Or is it more like the low mood comes and goes throughout each day or week?\n\nPatient: Um, well, when it hits, it usually sticks around for a while. Like... maybe a few months at a stretch, if I'm being honest. It doesn't really come and go day to day. It just sort of settles in. \n\nI guess the main thing is... well, I just stop doing the things I usually love. Like, I used to really look forward to grilling on the weekends, or going indoor climbing, or playing music at church. I even mentor the high school robotics kids, which I normally think is just the best thing. But during those long stretches, I just... I kind of stop caring about any of it. I'll see their messages asking for help or asking to hang out, and I just let them sit there unanswered. \n\nI feel terrible admitting that, because I know people are counting on me and I really don't want to let anyone down. I know I should just push through and go anyway. Does that make sense? I hope that's not too unusual or... or disappointing to hear.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those low periods that lasts a few months, is that one continuous stretch of feeling down, or does it break up into separate episodes with some better days or weeks in between?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess I'm not entirely sure how to answer that. I mean, it's not like I'm tracking every single day on a calendar or anything. I try to stay positive and just keep moving forward, you know? \n\nI suppose... well, I don't really know if it's one long, unbroken stretch or if there are little breaks in there. I'd like to think I have some good days mixed in, because I really do try to snap out of it. I don't want to be difficult or give you the wrong impression. I just... I haven't really paid that close attention to whether it's continuous or broken up. Is it okay if I don't have a perfectly clear answer for that one? I want to be helpful, I just don't want to guess and tell you the wrong thing.\n\nDoctor: When you think back over the past year or two, can you describe how many separate times you've had one of those low, flat periods? Like, how many different times did it start and then eventually lift?\n\nPatient: Um, gosh... I really haven't kept a strict count. I mean, I try not to dwell on the negative times too much, so I just sort of push past them when they lift. \n\nIf I had to guess... I don't know, maybe a few times? But honestly, I'm just not certain how many separate times it's happened over the last year or two. I really wish I had a better answer for you. I should probably be keeping a journal or something, shouldn't I? I just... I haven't really tracked the exact number of times. Is it alright if I just say I'm not really sure?\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those high-energy, really driven periods you mentioned at the beginning—where you're launching lots of projects and feeling very motivated—how much sleep do you typically need during those times? Do you feel rested on just a few hours of sleep, or do you still feel like you need your normal amount?\n\nPatient: Well, um, when I get like that, I actually don't need a whole lot of sleep. I hope that doesn't sound too strange. But, yeah, there have been these runs where I'll sleep maybe... maybe three hours? And then I'll wake up sharp at 3 or 4 a.m., and I don't feel tired at all. It's actually kind of nice, like I've been handed bonus time to get things done, you know? \n\nI know you're supposed to get eight hours, and I really do try to have good habits most of the time. But during those stretches, I just feel completely rested on those few hours. Is that... is that something I should be worried about? I just want to make sure I'm not doing anything wrong by taking advantage of the extra time.\n\nDoctor: During one of those low periods you mentioned, when it does eventually lift and you start feeling better, how quickly does that happen? Does the mood improve over a day or two, or does it take longer—like a week or more—before you feel like yourself again?\n\nPatient: Um, well, when it starts to get better... I guess I haven't really timed it exactly. I try to just be grateful when the fog starts lifting, you know? \n\nI don't think it's like flipping a switch in a day or two, but... I also don't want to make it sound like it drags on forever once it starts improving. Maybe it's somewhere in the middle? I just sort of realize one day that things feel a little lighter, and I try to focus on that instead of analyzing how long it took. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's another vague answer. I really am trying to give you the information you need. Is it okay if I'm just not totally sure how fast the transition is? I hope I'm not being too frustrating.\n\nDoctor: When one of those low periods starts to lift, and you begin feeling better, would you say it takes at least two weeks before you're back to feeling like your normal self again?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm really not sure if it takes a full two weeks. I mean, once I start feeling a bit better, I just try to focus on that positive momentum, you know? I don't really mark it on a calendar or anything. \n\nI guess it could take that long sometimes, but I wouldn't want to say for sure and give you the wrong idea. I just... I try to be happy that it's going away. Does that make sense? I hope it's okay that I don't have a more exact timeline for you. I'm really trying to be helpful, I promise.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to the last time you were in one of those low, flat periods, roughly how many weeks did that particular stretch last before you started feeling better?\n\nPatient: Um, well, the last time... I guess if you add up all the time I've spent in those really low, withdrawn periods over the whole course of this... it's probably been around seven months total. \n\nI know that sounds like a really long time, and I don't want you to think I'm just wallowing in it or not trying to get better. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, well... if I think about the whole time this has been going on... I guess if you add all those low periods together, it probably comes out to around seven months total.

I hope that doesn't sound too strange. But, yeah, there have been these runs where I'll sleep maybe... maybe three hours? And then I'll wake up sharp at 3 or 4 a.m., and I don't feel tired at all. But during those stretches, I just feel completely rested on those few hours. Is that... is that something I should be worried about? I just want to make sure I'm not doing anything wrong by taking advantage of the extra time.\n\nDoctor: During one of those low periods you mentioned, when it does eventually lift and you start feeling better, how quickly does that happen? Does the mood improve over a day or two, or does it take longer—like a week or more—before you feel like yourself again?\n\nPatient: Um, well, when it starts to get better... I guess I haven't really timed it exactly. I try to just be grateful when the fog starts lifting, you know? \n\nI don't think it's like flipping a switch in a day or two, but... I also don't want to make it sound like it drags on forever once it starts improving. Maybe it's somewhere in the middle? I just sort of realize one day that things feel a little lighter, and I try to focus on that instead of analyzing how long it took. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's another vague answer. I really am trying to give you the information you need. Is it okay if I'm just not totally sure how fast the transition is? I hope I'm not being too frustrating.\n\nDoctor: When one of those low periods starts to lift, and you begin feeling better, would you say it takes at least two weeks before you're back to feeling like your normal self again?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm really not sure if it takes a full two weeks. I mean, once I start feeling a bit better, I just try to focus on that positive momentum, you know? I don't really mark it on a calendar or anything. \n\nI guess it could take that long sometimes, but I wouldn't want to say for sure and give you the wrong idea. I just... I try to be happy that it's going away. Does that make sense? I hope it's okay that I don't have a more exact timeline for you. I'm really trying to be helpful, I promise.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to the last time you were in one of those low, flat periods, roughly how many weeks did that particular stretch last before you started feeling better?\n\nPatient: Um, well, the last time... I really do try to push through it. It's just that sometimes it's so hard to shake. \n\nIs that... is seven months a really bad sign? I hope I'm not a hopeless case or anything. Like, sometimes it feels like it's completely gone, and I'm doing great, but other times it might just be sort of... hovering a little bit? Just in the background. I really don't think it's always there underneath. I mean, when I'm feeling better, I really do feel good! ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, gosh, that's a really good question... I'm sorry, I don't actually know the exact answer to that.

I mean, I try not to dwell on the negative times too much, so I just sort of push past them when they lift. \n\nIf I had to guess... I don't know, maybe a few times? I should probably be keeping a journal or something, shouldn't I? I just... I haven't really tracked the exact number of times. I also don't want to make it sound like it drags on forever once it starts improving. Maybe it's somewhere in the middle? I just sort of realize one day that things feel a little lighter, and I try to focus on that instead of analyzing how long it took. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's another vague answer. Um, well, when I'm out of the low periods, I definitely feel, um, very productive. Like I said, I really try to make up for lost time.
